# Daniel Iles — notes on every video

One section per video, newest first (channel order): summary, key points with timestamp links, short quotes, and how each applies to G&L M&A Advisory. Source: YouTube auto-captions, read in full. Paraphrased; quotes kept short.

## This is how I run ads to make an insane amount of profit every month — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk
**Gist:** For high-ticket lead generation, Meta's purchase optimisation lacks data, so don't trust cheap cost per call. Combine Meta spend data, SQLs from the CRM and closed sales per ad, using last-click attribution, then manually shift budget toward ads with high SQL rates and ROAS.
**Nuggets:**
- `[ADS]` Cheap clicks, leads and booked calls can hide no-shows and unqualified prospects. Often the cheaper the call, the worse the quality. ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=0s))
- `[ADS]` Benchmark: just over $1M/mo on Facebook generated over $5M in revenue from ads. ([1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=61s))
- `[KPIS]` Go deeper than cost per call to cost per SQL, a sales-qualified lead that the rep confirms is real and able to buy, whether or not it closes. The ideal is to build around actual buyers. ([1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=61s))
- `[ADS]` Meta best practices are built for e-commerce (many fast purchases). High-ticket lead gen has few purchases (e.g. $5K spend with no sale) and about a week's lag from booking to buying, so the pixel can't optimise on purchases. ([2:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=122s))
- `[ADS]` Structure: one ad per ad set, so spend, cost per result and results are visible per creative. ([3:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=184s))
- `[TOOLS]` Combine three data sources for the same period: (1) the Meta export (spend and booked calls per ad), (2) SQLs per ad from a CRM such as GoHighLevel with last-click attribution, (3) sales and revenue per ad. Merge them with an LLM prompt into one table: ad, spend, calls, cost per call, SQLs, cost per SQL, sales, revenue, ROAS. ([3:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=214s))
- `[KPIS]` There are roughly 3–5× more SQLs than sales, so SQL is the middle ground with enough data to act on. ([4:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=245s))
- `[ADS]` Budget rules from the table: an ad at 9× ROAS (six sales, the video's hook ad) goes from $3K to $4K/day. One at 5× ROAS goes from $2K to $1.5K/day. One at 2.7× ROAS goes from $1K to $500/day or off, even though its calls are cheap. ([5:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=307s))
- `[ADS]` Why it works: Meta decides who sees an ad and how many, but you decide where the budget goes. Moving spend to high-ROI, SQL-rich ads makes Meta find more of those people. Left alone, Meta favours cheap-call ads. ([6:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=369s))
- `[KPIS]` Last-click attribution (what they saw right before booking) has been the most reliable signal across about $10M of spend. First-click is messy to track. ([7:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=430s))
- `[KPIS]` At low spend, rely on SQLs rather than sparse sales. An ad with eight SQLs is more trustworthy than one with two. Watch the SQL-to-sale ratio: 4:1 is weak, 2:2 means every SQL bought. ([7:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=460s))
- `[ADS]` Never scale on a single sale. Wait for a second or third sale and statistically meaningful ROAS. Use the table to kill ads that generate spam calls Meta can't tell apart from good ones. ([8:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=521s))
**Short quotes:**
- "The cheaper the call, the worse the [quality]." ([1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=61s))
- "Facebook controls the number of calls... But you control the quality of the appointments." ([7:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8qNXh62axk&t=430s))
- *Captions say "ice" and "ROROWAS". These almost certainly mean "quality" and "ROAS".*
**G&L angle:**
- Run G&L Meta ads one ad per ad set. Have reps mark SQL in the CRM after each call, e.g. buyer with liquidity, credit and a realistic timeline, or seller with revenue/EBITDA in range and an exit horizon.
- Build a weekly LLM-merged table (Meta export + CRM SQLs + closed deals by last-click ad) and move budget by ROAS and SQL rate, not cost per call.
- G&L's sales cycle may be longer than a week, so lean on SQL as the main optimisation signal and use closed revenue as confirmation.
- Cheap "no money down" curiosity calls are the predictable trap. Cut ads with low SQL rates even when the calls are cheap.

## Focus on revenue — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg
**Gist:** Every revenue jump (to $500K, $1.3M, $3M, then $5M/mo) came from understanding where revenue comes from, how people want to pay, where it leaks, and which ads actually drive ROAS. Revenue growth makes most other problems irrelevant.
**Nuggets:**
- `[KPIS]` At about $500K/mo, organic produced 5–10% of leads but up to 60% of revenue. Paid produced 90% of leads, filled calendars, and closed poorly. Measure closed revenue by source, not just bookings. ([0:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=30s))
- `[ADS]` Filling reps' calendars with cold calls that don't buy is pointless. Fix how cold traffic converts first, then scale. ([1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=61s))
- `[SCALING-STAGES]` $500K → $1.3M/mo: work out which ads turn leads into revenue rather than just leads. ([2:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=121s))
- `[PRICING]` $1.3M → $3M/mo (+$1.7M in 30 days): switch from MRR to package pricing. Collect as much as possible upfront and put the rest on an instalment plan. ([2:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=121s))
- `[GUARANTEE]` $3–4M → $5M/mo: find where revenue leaks. Refunds under the 7-day trial guarantee were draining cash. Understanding why some clients stayed and others refunded added almost $2M in gross revenue in one month. ([3:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=183s))
- `[CREATIVE]` Analyse real ROAS by ad format, not cost per call. Static images did worse on ROAS than expanded case-study ads, and focusing on this improved marketing efficiency by 50–60%. ([3:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=214s))
- `[PROFIT]` If spend stays flat and revenue rises, the gain goes straight to profit. Costs can only be cut so far, but revenue has no ceiling. Cutting costs 10% is easier than growing revenue 20% to cover them. ([4:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=245s))
- `[PROFIT]` Taking 10% off the top of a $1M/mo business ($100K/mo) is easier than running a $100K/mo business at 100% margin. ([5:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=336s))
- `[MINDSET]` Doubling revenue in 30 days makes website, UGC and back-office worries irrelevant. Focus on where revenue comes from, how to get more of it, and how to keep it. ([5:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=306s))
**Short quotes:**
- "There is a limit to how much you can reduce costs... but there is an infinite amount of income you can generate." ([4:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=275s))
- "The only thing that matters is income." ([5:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_oZlUE9Eygg&t=306s))
**G&L angle:**
- Build a closed-revenue-by-source report for G&L (organic, referral, Meta ad ID, YouTube) rather than relying on cost per booked call.
- Test case-study creatives (e.g. "how X bought a $3M business with 10% down via vendor finance") against static images on ROAS.
- If G&L offers a refund window, track refund and cancellation reasons per cohort and fix onboarding to plug the leak.
- Package pricing: big upfront, the remainder on a plan. Avoid monthly billing.

## I paid 20k to learn this in 5 minutes — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM
**Gist:** Switching from monthly-retainer (MRR) billing to selling an outcome with large upfront payment took Viral Coach from $1.3M to $3M/mo within 30 days. Value is delivered early, so payment should be front-loaded too. Upfront cash also funds acquisition, makes maintenance upsells easy, and attracts better clients.
**Nuggets:**
- `[PRICING]` Flat monthly fees for a service are the wrong model. Moving to outcome-based, upfront pricing took them from $1.3M to $3M/mo within 30 days. ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=0s))
- `[PRICING]` Book analogy: nobody wants to keep paying for a book they have already read. Match payment to when the value is delivered: maximum value and maximum payment at the start. ([0:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=30s))
- `[FULFILLMENT]` The personal-training lesson: clients get most of the value (setup, system, training) in the first weeks and then naturally want to cut out the middleman. That churn comes from the billing model, not from service quality. ([1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=92s))
- `[PRICING]` People will pay $10K once more readily than $1K/mo for 24 months. Nearly all 24-month payers leave before the end. ([3:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=183s))
- `[FINANCE]` Upfront cash funds customer acquisition. SaaS example: $100/mo × 24 months = $2,400 LTV, so $1,000 CAC is justified, but it takes about 10 months to recoup each customer, which stalls ad scaling without VC money. ([3:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=213s))
- `[GUARANTEE]` Price on an outcome rather than a time period, e.g. a guaranteed million views, or defined ad results within six months. ([4:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=274s))
- `[OFFER]` Prepayment makes maintenance upsells easy. After $30K upfront, a $500/week maintenance plan feels small next to protecting what they already paid for, the way a $3K security system leads to a $30/mo monitoring contract. ([5:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=304s))
- `[FINANCE]` Upfront billing means the client covers onboarding and acquisition cost in month 1–2 instead of you waiting 5–6 months to recoup. ([6:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=365s))
- `[PRICING]` "I'll get fewer clients" is actually an advantage. A higher entry bar brings wealthier, more business-savvy clients who participate more and get better results. ([6:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=395s))
- `[PROFIT]` Double the price with half the clients gives the same revenue at half the delivery cost, so a much higher margin. Big companies move upmarket for this reason. ([7:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=426s))
**Short quotes:**
- "You want to sell a result, not just a subscription. Nobody likes to buy a monthly subscription." ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=0s))
- "Maximum value at the start, maximum payment at the start." ([1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=61s))
**G&L angle:**
- Don't sell G&L as a monthly retainer. Price a defined outcome (e.g. "acquisition program to signed LOI/closing" or "exit-readiness to market-ready") with as much as possible upfront and the rest in instalments.
- Upfront cash funds the Meta/VSL machine. Track how many days it takes to recoup CAC.
- After a large upfront fee, sell low-cost ongoing services: post-acquisition advisory/board seat, monthly deal-flow access, value-protection retainer for sellers.
- Raise the price and accept fewer clients. Buyers with more liquidity and owners with real EBITDA close more deals, which produces better case studies.
- Success fees on closing fit the "sell the outcome" principle as a back-end.

## The UGC system behind thousands of our facebook ads — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI
**Gist:** Daniel's system for producing about 500 UGC ads a month at about $35 each. Start from a script the founder has already proven on camera, cast many paid-trial actors on Backstage.com, send a clear brief and ICP document, and keep the creators who perform.
**Nuggets:**
- `[UGC]` Scale benchmark: about 500 UGC ads a month at about $30 per video plus about $5 for subtitle editing, so about $35 per creative. 100 ads cost a few thousand dollars, and more creative variety improves ROAS. ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=0s))
- `[CREATIVE]` Start with a control script you have read on camera yourself and proven with Meta spend. Otherwise you can't tell whether a flop came from the script or the actor. ([0:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=30s))
- `[TOOLS]` Source talent on backstage.com, a casting site full of LA actors. It is free to post, handles payments, and the talent looks good and is eager for work. ([1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=61s))
- `[UGC]` Each creator does a paid trial of five videos using your best scripts. Keep even imperfect ones for variety and offer long-term work to consistent winners at a higher rate on larger batches. ([1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=92s))
- `[UGC]` Start with at least 10 creators. It's a numbers game. 10–20+ people reading proven scripts gives a large, varied bank of validated creatives, so finding one star is a bonus. ([2:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=123s))
- `[UGC]` Job post: name the company, say scripts are provided and editing is done in-house (creators only film and send raw files; other approaches didn't work), require good production quality, state up front that it pays $30 per video as a trial with long-term potential, and link the ICP and demographic brief. ([3:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=214s))
- `[UGC]` The creative brief covers file-delivery instructions, the customer avatar and buying motivation, who you are, the parts of the offer that sell, why your company, and payment terms (via Backstage for the trial, then a contract). Sell the creator on your being a stable long-term partner. ([4:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=244s))
- `[UGC]` Reply template to applicants: you liked their application, you're expanding the long-term partner base, trial of 5–15 videos at $30 each, the quality expected, "let us know if this is a good fit." Most can shoot five in a day, and anyone who doesn't deliver the same day is probably not a long-term fit. ([4:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=275s))
- `[UGC]` Remove guesswork: send example videos (ideally your own, to show tone, pace and delivery), a publicly openable script doc, and a Google Drive upload link. ([5:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=305s))
- `[UGC]` 50 creators × 5–15 videos gives a large pre-validated creative bank without much test budget, because the script is already proven. Prerequisite: an ad that already returns 4–8× ROAS at launch. ([6:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=367s))
**Short quotes:**
- "If you don't know if it's the script or the actor, you can't really come up with a winning option." ([0:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=30s))
- "It's really a numbers game." ([2:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=y0SVI--8iDI&t=153s))
**G&L angle:**
- Bendik records the control acquisition and exit scripts himself first and proves them on Meta. Only then hire UGC talent.
- Cast age-appropriate actors on Backstage: 40–55 "corporate professional buying a business" for buy-side, and 55+ "owner planning an exit" for sell-side. Brief them with the G&L ICP document and the vendor-finance angle.
- Trial 10–20 creators × five videos at about $30–35 each, so roughly $2–3.5K for 50–100 validated variants to feed the one-ad-per-ad-set structure.
- Watch compliance in scripts: actors should not claim to be real clients or make specific financial outcome promises.

## Become the best version overnight — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk
**Gist:** You can only become uncontested by buying out your competitors or by narrowing your niche and service until you are the only one doing exactly that thing. Viral Coach won by defining a hyper-specific category, then expanding it vertical by vertical with case studies.
**Nuggets:**
- `[OFFER]` Two ways to have no competitors: buy them all (expensive), or narrow the market and service until you are the only one doing exactly this for exactly these people. ([0:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=20s))
- `[OFFER]` Their niche definition: social media marketing for traditional business owners with $1M+ annual revenue who need short-form content (Reels, TikTok, Shorts) and have only a few hours a month. ([1:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=82s))
- `[OBJECTIONS]` When prospects say "I'll compare with others", ask "Who else are you talking to who does the same thing?" In a narrow niche the answer is no one. ([1:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=112s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Lots of "let me think about it" or comparison objections usually signal that the positioning is too broad. ([1:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=112s))
- `[OFFER]` Being second is worthless because clients want the best. Choose the category ("drawer") where you are first instead of letting the client file you with everyone else. ([2:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=174s))
- `[CASE-STUDIES]` After owning one niche, expand vertically: best for lawyers, doctors, home care, each with its own case studies. Then claim the broader category (best at short-form content). ([3:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=205s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` They show competitors to prospects on the call ("this is #2, #3, #4") because the comparison clearly favours them. They never compare themselves with the giant generalist firms. ([3:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=236s))
- `[FULFILLMENT]` Specialisation compounds. One clearly defined service for one type of client gets faster with every client instead of each new client bringing new nuances. ([4:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=266s))
**Short quotes:**
- "In business, being second is absolutely pointless. You have to be the best." ([2:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=174s))
- "We chose the criteria ourselves and called ourselves the best because we were the only ones who did." ([5:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=326s))
**G&L angle:**
- Define G&L's category narrowly enough to be the only one in it. Buy side: "vendor-finance acquisition advisory for corporate professionals buying their first $1–5M EBITDA business". Sell side: "exit-readiness for founders 55+ with no successor". That is not "M&A advisory" in general.
- Use "Who else are you talking to who does exactly this?" against business-broker and generic-coach comparisons.
- Build vertical case studies (e.g. trades, healthcare services, B2B services) and then claim each vertical.
- A standardised deal process (sourcing → valuation → structuring → close) gets faster with every client.

## $5M/month secrets for selling cold traffic — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU
**Gist:** Colder traffic has less trust and less margin for error, so Daniel gives three rules: anchor on what prospects already believe (don't sell new beliefs), use a money-back guarantee that lets them "stay at rest" while still buying, and eliminate every hidden or add-on fee by bundling into one price. Each layer of colder traffic (at $500k, $1M, $1.5M, $3M/month) surfaced new friction over ever-smaller extra costs.
**Nuggets:**
- `[SALES-CALL]` Cold prospects don't know/like/trust you — you can't win a "world-bending" belief change; sell them on what they already believe and do. ([0:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=30s))
- `[OBJECTIONS]` Anchor #1 — the hour they already invested: when asked "prove why I should do this," flip it: "You've already invested an hour of your valuable time — what about our ads, landing page or your business made you believe it was worth it?" ([1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=92s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Anchor #2 — their existing success: ask "What percentage of calls you get on do you close?" They brag about closing 90% of referrals → "Then we just need to get you on the phone with more people." ([2:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=154s))
- `[OBJECTIONS]` "Sounds good, let me think about it" has no objection to handle and lost them huge revenue; colder audiences avoid action — don't try to force the object at rest into motion. ([3:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=216s))
- `[GUARANTEE]` Redefine "staying at rest": offer a call-only refundable deposit/money-back trial so they keep "thinking about it" with the real team. Script: you're busy; next week I won't have anything new and you won't have better information — let our team prove it; if you don't like it, tell them and get a full refund. ([4:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=279s))
- `[GUARANTEE]` Declining a risk-free, on-this-call-only option becomes the active (negative) decision — crossing you off — which option-keepers dislike, so they default to trying. Then inertia keeps them through onboarding long enough to deliver results. ([5:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=340s))
- `[PRICING]` People hate hidden/additional costs disproportionately more than a higher price (all-inclusive hotel vs. $20 minibar water). ([6:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=401s))
- `[OFFER]` Hidden-fee friction scales with traffic coldness: ~$3k gear requirement was fine until ~$500k/month → cut to ~$300 kit → by ~$1M/month even $300 felt like a scam, so they ship gear free. ([8:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=495s))
- `[OFFER]` At ~$1.3–1.5M/month an $80/month posting tool caused "upsell/affiliate scam" complaints → enterprise deal to include it; at ~$3M/month an $8/month Slack Pro fee (90 days in) lost contracts → enterprise Slack agreement costing tens of thousands. ([9:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=556s))
- `[PRICING]` Bundle required add-ons (e.g., a client's $4k/month ad spend) into one all-in price — less sales resistance. ([10:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=650s))
- `[OFFER]` Cold traffic needs less complexity everywhere — sales process, engagement and delivery — and everything explicitly stated up front so nothing new surprises them later. ([11:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=681s))
**Short quotes:**
- "You can't sell them on new beliefs. You can only sell what they already believe." ([10:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=650s))
- "You can only have them commit to not committing, but you can still win." ([11:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=681s))
**G&L angle:**
- Anchor buyers on beliefs they already hold ("you already know owning a cash-flowing business beats a job/real estate") and sellers on theirs ("you've built something valuable; you've already decided to explore an exit by booking this call") rather than teaching seller finance from scratch.
- Ask business-owner sellers what they're already good at (operations, customer relationships) and frame G&L as more of what works for them.
- Offer a refundable deposit/trial that starts the engagement with an advisor now, available only on the call, to beat "let me think about it."
- Price all-inclusive: fold in valuation reports, data-room tools, legal templates, deal-sourcing databases/CRM access so there are no surprise extras; state any success fees or third-party costs (lawyers, accountants, lender fees) explicitly up front.

## $5M/mo objection overcomes — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08
**Gist:** Daniel's favourite word tracks for cold-traffic objections: "let me think about it," lack of trust, needing to ask a partner, "prove you know my industry," "let me call a client," "results first, then pay," and "show me a case study exactly like me." He adds tracking lost deals by BANT category, copying the rep who never gets a given objection, conviction-driven tone, and survivorship bias in the objections you never hear.
**Nuggets:**
- `[OBJECTIONS]` "Sounds good, let me think on it" (no real objection): if everything you offered isn't enough, offer your absence — "Assuming this doesn't work out, what's the plan to get this done by yourself?" Not having a plan is the reason to buy. ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=0s))
- `[GUARANTEE]` Lack of trust ("I just saw you on an ad yesterday") is handled with a money-back guarantee, which added a couple of million dollars a month. ([0:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=30s))
- `[OBJECTIONS]` "I need more information": agree with them, then flip it — the only way to get more info is to take the next step (a deposit) and talk to the fulfillment team, not spend another hour with a sales rep. "I need to find out more before I buy" becomes "If I want to find out more, I need to buy." ([1:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=60s))
- `[OBJECTIONS]` "I need to run this by my partner/wife": take a refundable deposit now, give risk-free access to the materials to show them, and invite them to the onboarding/strategy call to ask their questions. ([1:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=90s))
- `[OBJECTIONS]` "Prove you're an expert in my industry": don't try — you'll never out-know a doctor about medicine. Reframe: "You're the expert in your industry; we're the experts in marketing — we need your raw knowledge to package it in a way that sells." ([1:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=90s))
- `[OBJECTIONS]` "Let me call one of your clients": never hand out client contacts (disrespectful, sometimes illegal). Experiment: a pro-sales-trainer client took calls with 15 prospects who said a client call would make them "100% in" — only 1 signed. It's an exit excuse, not a proof request. Line: "I can't give you my clients' contact info, but I promise I won't give yours out either — I won't make you my sales rep." ([2:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=121s))
- `[OBJECTIONS]` "Get me results first, then I'll pay": defend hard or end the call. Point to 1,000+ clients and five-star reviews, then reframe the real question — not "does this work?" but "will it work for me?", which depends on their belief in themselves; end optimistically ("you've bet on yourself before and turned out fine"). ([3:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=215s))
- `[CASE-STUDIES]` "Show me a case study exactly like me" is usually a moving target: he met every escalating condition for a sports-betting prospect (100M-view account, TikTok and IG, follower growth, before/after) and the prospect still ghosted after 3 months of follow-up. ([4:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=276s))
- `[CASE-STUDIES]` Two better responses: (1) for new founders, "we don't have one like you — let's make you the first in your space" (novelty/exclusivity; avoid with established owners who want proof); (2) show the closest case study but point out the *differences* ("it'd look different for you because…") so prospects argue the similarities themselves. ([6:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=398s))
- `[SALES-MGMT]` Tag every lost deal as budget, authority, need or timing. Find the category that loses the most deals, then find the rep who rarely gets it and copy them. Their fix for authority: open every call with "Who else from your team needs to be on this call?" and loop in or reschedule decision-makers — rolled out team-wide, everyone closed more. ([8:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=522s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Tone over tactics: if you believe your offer is the best option and the buyer will buy something anyway, sell like it matters so they don't buy the worse option — prospects hear that conviction. If you don't believe it's the best, go sell for someone else. ([9:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=584s))
- `[FUNNEL]` Survivorship bias (WWII planes): the most dangerous objections are those you never hear — e.g. "this looks like a scam" — because those people never book or show. Fix the website, testimonials and reminder emails for objections that stop people reaching the call. ([10:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=646s))
**Short quotes:**
- "If everything you offered was not enough, offer your absence." ([0:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=30s))
- "The most dangerous objections are the ones that you don't get." ([10:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=615s))
**G&L angle:**
- "Let me think about it" from a seller: "If we don't work together, what's your plan to get this sold — and to get paid for it — on your own, and by when?" For buyers: "What's the plan to find, finance and close a deal yourself?"
- Use a refundable deposit + invite the spouse/business partner/accountant to the onboarding call — authority objections are endemic with 55+ owners. Have closers ask "Who else is involved in this decision?" in the first minute and reschedule if a key person is missing.
- "Prove you know my industry": "You're the expert in your trade; we're the experts in structuring and selling businesses." Never hand out past clients' contacts (confidentiality matters even more in M&A).
- Show the nearest closed deal, highlighting differences (industry, size, structure) so the prospect builds the case for themselves; tag lost deals by BANT in the CRM.
- Fix the unspoken "scam?/too good to be true" objection around "no money down" before the call with credibility proof (named deals, advisors, press) on the page and in reminders.

## Why do you turn the music down when looking for your turn? LMAO — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M
**Gist:** A short mindset piece on attention. Like turning the music down to find your exit, focus needs undivided attention. The one handicap Daniel would wish on competitors is distraction (extra businesses, too many clients or employees, nightlife), because it turns all their resources against them. He lives in Alaska partly to avoid distractions.
**Nuggets:**
- `[MINDSET]` Split attention quietly undermines everything. You turn down the music to read road signs, and the same thing applies across business and life ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=0s))
- `[MINDSET]` A serious competitor can overcome expensive customers, a worse product, bad staff, thin margins or refunds with time and capital. The one handicap that beats them is distraction: multiple businesses, too many employees, too many clients ([0:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=31s), [1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=61s), [1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=92s))
- `[MINDSET]` People treat new opportunities (a second business, a course, masterminds) as positives, but every new opportunity is a guaranteed distraction whether or not it succeeds ([1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=92s), [3:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=186s))
- `[LIFE]` He lives in Alaska partly to cut distractions. Having little to do is what creates focus ([2:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=124s), [2:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=155s))
- `[LEADERSHIP]` Time, money and effort can be bought or delegated; attention is finite in the moment. Giving 20% elsewhere leaves you at 80% on your main goal. Still take vacations and leave time to think ([3:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=217s), [4:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=249s), [4:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=281s))
**Short quotes:**
- "I wish they all had five extra businesses cuz I'm the only one taking my one seriously." ([4:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=249s))
- "Attention is so scarce because it's one of the few things that is extremely finite." ([3:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a-0teS5cO6M&t=217s))
**G&L angle:**
- Guard against opportunity sprawl. With The Owners Academy, G&L advisory and personal acquisitions, decide which one gets 100% of founder attention this quarter and delegate or pause the rest.
- Prospects in a buy/sell process are distracted too, so use calls and the VSL to narrow their focus to one decision.

## I spent $1M on Facebook ads - here is what actually works (ROAS Breakdown) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y
**Gist:** Using 90 days of his own B2B call-funnel Meta data, Daniel ranks ad formats from worst to best by ROAS rather than cost per call: statics, B-roll text, founder talking heads, UGC talking heads and long-form case studies. He explains why a blend of formats beats putting everything on the top performer.
**Nuggets:**
- `[KPIS]` Optimise for ROAS, not cost per booked call. Cheap-call formats often bring spam or low-intent leads who don't show or buy. Letting Meta optimise only for "booked a call" pushes spend toward cheap, bad calls ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=0s), [0:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=31s))
- `[KPIS]` Track SQLs: the rep flags that a lead showed and was qualified, and you divide spend by SQLs to get cost per SQL ([2:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=123s))
- `[CREATIVE]` Statics and carousels ($90k over 90 days) had the cheapest cost per call at $424 but expensive cost per SQL and low ROAS. Cheap placements reach lower-intent people, who say "I saw one image yesterday, I'm not dropping $30–50k". He is scaling them back ([2:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=123s), [3:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=184s))
- `[CREATIVE]` B-roll plus text-on-screen ads look like organic content, so people read half of one before realising it's an ad. Footage congruent with the ICP works best (e.g. a producer setting up an interview shoot). They're great for top of funnel and as bottom-of-funnel reminder or retargeting ads, but they don't build trust alone, so don't put the whole budget on them ([3:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=215s), [4:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=245s), [5:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=307s))
- `[CREATIVE]` Founder-led talking heads have the highest ROAS: higher cost per call but lower cost per SQL. If sales complains about lead quality, run more of these. You need real presentation skill ([6:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=368s), [6:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=400s))
- `[ADS]` Performance marketing means blending formats. Turning off B-roll ads dropped account-wide ROAS. Put smaller budgets on weaker formats but keep them all running. Cutting an asset class's budget usually improves its efficiency because it reaches fewer, better-fit people ([7:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=430s), [7:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=462s), [8:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=492s))
- `[UGC]` UGC talking heads bring easy calls but higher cost per SQL. Some prospects booked because they liked an attractive actor and were let down by the rep. About $750k went into UGC in 90 days, mostly testing, which pulled ROAS down ([8:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=523s), [9:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=553s))
- `[UGC]` Test one variable at a time: use proven scripts while testing creators, then test new scripts on a proven creator ([9:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=584s))
- `[CASE-STUDIES]` Long-form Loom-style case-study ads (3–10 minutes) have high cost per call and per SQL, but prospects show up "with their credit card out". Most six-figure and enterprise clients came from these, so spend on them is increasing ([9:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=584s), [10:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=616s), [10:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=647s))
- `[CREATIVE]` AI-generated ads tested worse than statics ("no one likes AI slop") ([10:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=647s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Ad metrics only matter if the sales call closes. A $500 cost per call is good only with sales behind it ([11:17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=677s))
**Short quotes:**
- "If your sales team is complaining about lead quality, you need more of these." ([6:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=400s))
- "Creative diversity is what makes the whole thing work." ([8:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PVwA6gA633Y&t=492s))
**G&L angle:**
- Judge G&L ads by cost per SQL and cash ROAS, not cost per booked call. Have closers tag SQLs in the CRM and feed that event to Meta.
- Make long-form case-study ads a priority: 3–10 minute walkthroughs of a specific vendor-financed acquisition or an owner's exit (numbers, structure, timeline). They are likely to attract the highest-ticket clients.
- Founder talking heads from Bendik/Josh should be the core ROAS driver. Add B-roll/text ads (deal-room footage, business walkthroughs, handshake at settlement) for reach and retargeting.
- Make sure UGC presenters are congruent with who prospects will meet on the call, or the mismatch costs SQLs.

## Every revenue level from $10k to $5M / month explained (51 minute breakdown) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0
**Gist:** Daniel walks through Viral Coach's path from zero to $5M/month: copying a proven ads → call funnel → high-ticket program model, nearly going broke scaling unprofitable ad spend, then two single "unlocks" (switching from weekly billing to 6-month paid-upfront, and tightening the 7-day money-back trial) that each roughly doubled/tripled revenue within 30 days. Core themes: know your customer better than they know themselves, systematize so labor cost per revenue dollar falls, and buy experience rather than grinding alone.
**Nuggets:**
- `[MINDSET]` Don't reinvent the model — he bought a $15k course (Cole Gordon) exposing a working ads → call funnel → high-ticket multi-month program business and copy-pasted it; took ~4–6 months of burning cash before real revenue. ([5:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=309s))
- `[SCALING-STAGES]` Month one did $120k on a ~$50k ad budget, ~$30–40k net, with the founder closing every call himself. ([7:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=432s))
- `[PRICING]` Start underpriced so the offer converts easily on cold traffic; once you have proof it sells on a call, double the price — then double again, and again. ([7:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=465s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Founder should stay on the phones longer than feels comfortable — he left sales at month 4–5 and thinks he should have stayed a full year; first three months on calls were formative. ([8:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=526s))
- `[ADS]` Scaling ad spend before the unit economics work just loses money faster: he scaled to a couple hundred thousand/month in spend at month 3, ended month 6 with $500k of unsecured debt and two months "in the pit" checking daily cash to decide whether to keep ads on. ([9:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=588s))
- `[SALES-HIRING]` At $500k/month he had ~10 weak sales reps; today any single rep closing under $500k/month is underperforming — skill gap matters more than headcount. ([11:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=714s))
- `[TEAM]` Bloat is natural the first time you hit a new level — you over-expand, then contract and get lean. ([12:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=775s))
- `[OPERATIONS]` As you scale, average pay per employee should go down, not up: systems, SOPs, tutorials and tooling make jobs easier (the McDonald's principle), so labor cost per revenue dollar falls. ([12:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=775s))
- `[SCALING-STAGES]` A profitable business at $100–500k/month can usually reach $1M/month just by cranking ad spend (~$300–400k/month spend); the real bottleneck is nuanced, non-plug-and-play fulfillment. Test: "Could you double sales right now and have fulfillment be okay?" ([14:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=868s))
- `[PRICING]` Know your customer better than they know themselves: if clients stay ~4–5 months, sell a 6-month contract (+20–30% topline); or raise price 50–100% so you fulfil for fewer clients at the same revenue. ([15:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=931s))
- `[FULFILLMENT]` In a service business a good result isn't enough — clients often can't recognize a good result, so you must deliver it, show them why it's good, and wrap it in customer service; plan what support they need in month 1 vs month 6. ([16:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=993s))
- `[OFFER]` Hyper-custom, unsystematized delivery only works in enterprise (PwC/EY-style $M engagements with brand reputation); if you're acquiring via cold Facebook ads, you must systematize and win on volume. ([17:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1055s))
- `[PROFIT]` Take ~10% of topline as profit and reinvest the rest — it's equally easy at $100k, $500k or $5M/month; he took essentially nothing for two years. "Boxer that doesn't need oxygen": competitors who pull cash for toys lose by attrition. ([19:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1181s))
- `[CREATIVE]` Under $1M/month, messaging/format/creative testing barely matters — at ~$10k/day you're only reaching the most responsive slice of the audience; even "slop"/AI ads convert. ([21:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1275s))
- `[MINDSET]` Buy experience: each big jump ($500k→$1M, $1.3M→$3M in 30 days, $3–4M→$5M in 30 days) came from one outside insight, not from more hours or staring at numbers. Find people richer/further ahead and pay for their perspective. ([23:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1400s))
- `[PRICING]` The $1.3M→$3M unlock (from a $20k one-hour consult): stop weekly/monthly "recurring" billing for a fixed program — clients equate payments with progress, and the second weekly charge (~$1,500) hit during onboarding before any visible result, causing rage and refunds. Charge the 6-month program upfront instead. ([26:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1618s))
- `[PRICING]` Set contract length from average churn of happy clients (~5 months → 6-month contract). Don't jump to 12 months: harder to sell, and keeping people past the value creates reviews/chargeback/complaint problems. ([30:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1834s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Switching to upfront payment needed no sales retraining or new reframes — the first call simply said "sounds good"; fewer buyers but far fewer refunds, so profit rose. ([32:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1925s))
- `[OPERATIONS]` While stuck at $1.3M for 5–6 months he "battened down the hatches": hundreds of ads live, a queue of trained account managers with SOPs, a staffed sales team with constant new reps — so when the unlock came nothing broke. ([33:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2017s))
- `[GUARANTEE]` 7-day money-back trial exists to beat the "no objection, I'll start next week" stall (cost them 30–40% of deals): "start today, and if anything changes before then you can back out." ([36:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2172s))
- `[GUARANTEE]` Failure mode: reps used the trial as a crutch for every objection, so clients came in unsold; many also took the overdelivered first-week roadmap and left to DIY. Fix ($3M→$5M): refund only by telling your account manager on the onboarding call (Planet Fitness logic) — refunds stopped. ([38:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2295s))
- `[FULFILLMENT]` The onboarding call is what makes people stick: a hyper-focused strategy built with automations/market research, delivered by a niche-experienced account manager. ([40:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2447s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Sales chooses which account manager a client goes to by industry expertise and personality fit; showing the prospect who's "on the other side of the fence" closes them even if they don't love the rep. ([42:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2540s))
- `[ADS]` Go broad before niching: niching early is stock-picking. They started targeting coaches/consultants (wrong — mostly broke, bad at selling) and found doctors, lawyers, accountants and finance pros by going broad first. ([44:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2663s))
- `[HIRING]` Almost no percentage-of-revenue commission comp anymore; pay fair-market/"bottom dollar you'd take" with room to ascend — now hiring similar roles at ~1/5 the early cost. ([46:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2786s))
- `[LEADERSHIP]` At $5M/month the CEO adds value through small high-leverage adjustments and partnerships, not by stepping into sales or fulfillment — "don't touch the steering wheel" at 100 mph. ([48:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2909s))
**Short quotes:**
- "As you scale, you should be paying your people less, not more." ([12:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=775s))
- "They attribute value and the price that they pay to the amount of progress that they make." ([28:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1739s))
**G&L angle:**
- Price the advisory as a fixed-term engagement collected upfront (or in large milestone tranches), not monthly/weekly — acquisition and exit-prep timelines are long and early months show little visible "progress," exactly the trap that caused refunds at Viral Coach.
- Set engagement length from data: how long until a typical buyer client closes a deal or a seller client is exit-ready; contract slightly beyond that.
- If offering a cooling-off/refund window, require it to be exercised live on the onboarding/strategy call with the assigned advisor, and coach closers never to use it as an objection crutch.
- Route new clients to the advisor with the closest industry/deal-type experience (e.g., trades, healthcare, e-commerce) and mention that advisor on the sales call.
- Before niching (e.g., only 55+ owners in one sector), run broad ads and let close/collection data reveal which owner/buyer segments are profitable.

## If you are serious about making generational wealth - you need to move — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8
**Gist:** A short motivational piece filmed on the site of Daniel's house build. Leaving his tiny hometown (North Pole, Alaska) and being exposed to wealth and successful people raised his "floor" of expectations. He urges viewers to spend at least two weeks a year somewhere new and inspiring, even on borrowed money.
**Nuggets:**
- `[LIFE]` Daniel credits moving away from his hometown of a few hundred people with almost everything he has achieved. Staying put is often just the default parents and peers set. ([0:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=1s), [1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=61s))
- `[MINDSET]` Environment shapes how you operate. Being in rooms full of millionaires changes your priorities, how you value time and how you think. Get around those people "no matter what it costs". ([2:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=123s))
- `[MINDSET]` Exposure raises your floor: seeing a penthouse, a yacht or a private jet even once resets what you consider the minimum standard, and you can't unsee it. ([3:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=185s), [4:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=245s))
- `[LIFE]` His concrete prescription: spend 2 weeks a year somewhere new (Greece, Miami, New York, LA). He even says to put $10K on a credit card to do it, and to expect pushback from family and friends. ([2:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=153s), [5:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=308s))
**Short quotes:**
- "You suddenly see a higher floor. You see a higher level of success as the minimum standard because you've been exposed to it." ([4:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=245s))
- "The purpose of growth is to be able to change." ([5:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yJFUKhvyJK8&t=308s))
**G&L angle:**
- Content angle for buyer ads: the "raise your floor" and environment-change story maps onto employees who have never pictured owning a business. Show them what ownership and acquisition entrepreneurs' lives look like.
- Use in-person events or deal-room days for clients to put aspiring acquirers in rooms with people who have already closed deals. Exposure raises their ambition and follow-through.
- For G&L's own team, invest in getting around higher-level M&A operators (masterminds, conferences) to raise the standard of what the firm considers normal.

## My $5 million per month sales system — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ
**Gist:** Daniel walks through the sales leaderboard behind $4.89M in new monthly sales: 2,671 leads → 39% DQ → 1,633 calls → ~48% show → ~800 shown calls. Everything is ranked by revenue per lead, stats are fully public to the team, lead tiers move monthly (even mid-month), and metrics like effective hourly rate and "efficiency cost" make coaching and lead allocation objective.
**Nuggets:**
- `[KPIS]` Funnel benchmarks at >$1M/month cold ad spend: 2,671 leads/month; ~39% DQ'd (spam/poor fit, often caught when reps call before the slot); 1,633 live calls; ~48% show rate (~800 shown). ([0:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=31s))
- `[SETTING]` Low show rates are normal on cold traffic — counter with double-booking, so a top rep can carry ~475 confirmed-looking calls per month. ([1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=92s))
- `[KPIS]` Revenue per lead is the master KPI (holds everything else constant); close rate can be measured too many ways and gamed. Leaderboard is sorted by revenue per lead. ([3:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=186s))
- `[SETTING]` Pre-call outreach drives shows: the top rep hit an 84% show rate (team 48%) by talking to prospects before the call, with a slightly below-average DQ rate (34% vs 39%). His pre-call messages are open for the team to copy. ([2:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=153s))
- `[SALES-MGMT]` Gamify: reset metrics monthly, move reps between color-coded lead tiers (worst/middle/best) even mid-month based on revenue per lead and total appointments taken. ([4:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=248s))
- `[SALES-MGMT]` Never let a rep's subjective "qualified" judgment affect their commission/standing. Use "qualified show rate" only as a confidence indicator: reps on identical tier-3 leads rated 33–87% qualified — those who believe leads are good close more. ([4:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=278s))
- `[SALES-MGMT]` Coach with hard facts: highlight anyone far below team average in red (e.g., over-DQ'ing) and let them copy the best performer, instead of subjective call nitpicking. ([7:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=433s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Different styles work: one rep pre-DQs aggressively ("entry price is this — is it even worth the discussion?") for a high show-to-close; another talks to anyone and wins on revenue per lead. Judge by revenue per lead, not show-to-close. ([7:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=464s))
- `[KPIS]` Leads-to-close ignores deal size when there are multiple price points — look at revenue produced. Top reps: $1.2M, $800k, $600k, $500k, $400k in a month. ([9:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=559s))
- `[SALES-HIRING]` Fewer, better reps: $500k–$1.2M/month can be done with one excellent rep with the right controls; 20–30 reps each closing ~$100k just creates commission, call-review and admin mess at 1/10 the efficiency. ([9:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=590s))
- `[SALES-MGMT]` Show month-over-month revenue-per-lead progress per rep — visible progress improves retention. ([10:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=652s))
- `[KPIS]` Effective hourly rate = commission month-to-date ÷ shown calls (≈$400–575/hour for top reps), plus a run-rate projection of monthly commission — shows reps what each extra call is worth. ([11:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=714s))
- `[SALES-MGMT]` Best reps take the most calls (more reps = more practice); route leads by priority and total availability. ([12:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=775s))
- `[SALES-HIRING]` Publicized earnings (e.g., a rep making $70k last month) create a huge talent pull — they nearly emptied a competitor's sales team. ([13:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=805s))
- `[KPIS]` "Efficiency cost": compare each rep's revenue per lead to the best in the same tier × their lead count → opportunity cost (e.g., $400k "lost"). Makes moving reps up/down objective and fair. ([14:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=865s))
- `[LEADERSHIP]` Publish the rules and data so the team can play the game with you; set standards by pointing to the best real performer, not made-up quotas. ([16:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=960s))
**Short quotes:**
- "The baseline metric that we make all of our decisions off of is revenue per lead." ([3:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=217s))
- "If you make the rules available to everyone, they can influence them. They can play this game with you." ([16:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MCPvsVEAvoQ&t=960s))
**G&L angle:**
- Build a G&L sales sheet: leads, DQ%, calls, show%, shown calls, revenue (cash collected) per lead, revenue per call, effective hourly rate — public to all closers, reset monthly.
- Have closers or setters call/text every booked G&L lead before the slot (confirm goals, deal size, capital, timeline) — the lever behind 84% vs 48% show.
- Tier leads from the application (e.g., seller with $1M+ EBITDA or buyer with liquidity and operator experience = tier 1) and give tier 1 to whoever has the highest revenue per lead.
- Allow double-booking on lower-tier slots to absorb no-shows from cold Meta traffic.
- Start lean: one or two strong closers can carry high revenue at G&L's price point — hire for quality, not headcount.

## $10k/mo vs $1m/mo paid ads — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ
**Gist:** Ten mistakes that separate ad accounts stuck under $10k/month from accounts scaling past $1M/month: CBO testing, creative volume and diversity (the A/T/G/C framework), formats, UGC, optimising to leading indicators, landing pages, the post-booking sales process, cold-traffic offers and guarantees, and contextual targeting.
**Nuggets:**
- `[ADS]` Don't test creatives in CBO. Meta may dump spend on a loser and give a potential winner nothing, so you never learn. Test one ad per ad set. He says this switch saved clients 30–50%. One client spending $500k/month was wasting 40% of spend on this mistake ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=0s), [0:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=31s), [1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=92s))
- `[CREATIVE]` Struggling accounts run 10–30 creatives with one message and one format, often filmed by the same person on the same day. Meta now matches hyper-specific content to hyper-specific audiences, so you need hundreds of ads across awareness levels, pain points and buyer looks ([2:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=122s), [2:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=153s))
- `[CREATIVE]` "DNA" ad framework: A = Audience (call out specific industries and their language), T = Talent (who reads it), G = Goal (clients vs more leads vs better leads), C = Container (packaging: leads, a million views, autopilot system, personal brand, staffed manager, paid ads, CRO). Swap one element at a time to get a new ad ([3:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=184s), [3:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=216s), [4:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=278s))
- `[UGC]` Having new talent read already-proven scripts skips testing spend. Adding female UGC creators cut costs by almost 30% ([4:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=246s))
- `[CREATIVE]` The content continuum: push one winning concept through written short, written long, short looping video, short talking head, long talking head, podcast and long training formats. The "I'll get you a million views or you don't pay" winner was made in ~100 variants (vertical, horizontal, looping, carousel, static) ([5:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=338s), [6:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=369s), [6:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=399s))
- `[UGC]` Each demographic needs its own spokesperson. A 45-year-old plumber and a 25-year-old entrepreneur respond to different faces. He hired a dozen UGC creators to read one proven script, which he calls one of the highest-leverage moves in company history ([7:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=461s), [8:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=522s))
- `[KPIS]` Don't optimise only for leading indicators like cost per lead or call. The same ads perform very differently across clients because of downstream factors: sales skill, price, deliverables, LTV and refunds. Diagnose the whole funnel ([9:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=553s), [10:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=615s))
- `[APPLICATION]` A new campaign cut cost per qualified call from $400 to $100 but flooded the calendar with 4x the volume of broke, non-showing leads. Without enough filtering, setter pre-qualification or funnel friction, it tanked a week of revenue (about $1M lost) ([10:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=646s), [11:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=676s))
- `[SCALING-STAGES]` The risk flips with scale. Early, the risk is moving too slowly. Later, it's changing something that wasn't broken, because the big business is a finely tuned instrument ([11:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=707s))
- `[LANDING-PAGE]` The 2020-style page (white background, headline, VSL, application) now signals an unprofessional business. A professional site redesign doubled lead conversions overnight and held for 30 days (compare viralcoach.com against the old site). Check page speed and mobile first, because a 1-point gain at the top of the funnel compounds downstream ([12:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=738s), [12:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=768s), [13:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=799s))
- `[SETTING]` Interruption-based cold traffic books calls cold. Contact every booking within 5 minutes by text, email and call. Then run an automated reminder sequence with case studies, testimonials, proof of work, founder videos and a pitch deck as pre-call material ([13:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=830s), [14:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=860s), [14:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=891s))
- `[OFFER]` A warm-traffic offer can't be scaled on cold traffic. When everything feels broken, it's usually an offer problem. Cold-traffic offers are easy to understand (one-page contract), have risk reversal, and are built around a one-call close ([14:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=891s), [15:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=953s), [16:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=984s))
- `[GUARANTEE]` Use a performance guarantee designed for cold traffic. Don't run a two-call close. Make it a blink-of-an-eye yes and work out the specifics after they're a client ([16:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=984s), [16:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=1015s))
- `[ADS]` Interest targeting is mostly useless now. Meta reads and transcribes your ad (words, on-screen text, visuals, background) to decide who sees it, which is contextual targeting. To reach Hormozi fans, name him in the script. Vague scripts are the most expensive ads; specific language and keywords make good leads cheaper ([16:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=1015s), [17:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=1078s), [18:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=1108s))
**Short quotes:**
- "The marketing is only ever as good as the company you're marketing." ([10:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=615s))
- "Your words are doing the targeting." ([18:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=1108s))
**G&L angle:**
- Build ads on A/T/G/C. Audience: retiring owners 55+, corporate escapees, tradies wanting to buy. Talent: an older owner-type presenter alongside a younger buyer-type. Goal: retire, buy income, exit at a higher multiple. Container: vendor finance, roll-up, exit-readiness.
- Use contextual targeting by naming the audience in the script, e.g. "if you own a business and you're over 55 and thinking about retiring...", or by referencing known names in the acquisition space.
- Test creatives one ad per ad set, not in CBO, then push winners through every format (static, carousel, looping text, talking head, long-form).
- Before cutting lead cost, make sure the application has enough friction (capital available, business revenue/EBITDA, timeline) and that setters pre-qualify, so cheap leads don't flood closers with unqualified buyers.
- Upgrade the G&L landing page beyond the 2020 white-page-VSL-form look and check mobile speed. Contact every booking within 5 minutes and send deal case studies, a founder video and a deck before the call.

## Explaining the $1m/mo offer — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis
**Gist:** Daniel interviews Jeremy Haynes on how his "Jeremy's Inner Circle" mastermind/coaching offer hit $1M/month recurring at $10K/month. Covers the offer stack, organic + word-of-mouth traffic, vetting/friction, closer accountability for bad-fit sales, laddered price increases, and using a public cap plus waitlist tiers to create urgency.
**Nuggets:**
- `[OFFER]` The offer was born from noticing an above-average buyer segment inside a lower offer who wouldn't talk openly among beginners. Build the premium tier for that group, originally sold as an upsell. ([1:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=63s))
- `[OFFER]` Stack: two 30-min 1:1s per month, a weekly group call (library of 180+ one-hour calls), quarterly in-person masterminds (live-streamed and recorded), SOPs, layered group chats (a general one plus a $1M+/month one), DM access, an AI clone ("Jeremy AI") and a content-finder bot. ([3:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=216s))
- `[FULFILLMENT]` Add a weekly group call once 1:1s and DMs keep repeating the same questions. Then document everything (SOPs, call library, AI clone) so only about 20% of members book 1:1s (30–40% at peak). That is what makes 1:1 access scale. ([2:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=156s), [9:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=587s))
- `[FULFILLMENT]` Curate the community hard. If one member complains about another's behaviour and a second person complains too, the offender is removed and refunded. Group quality is part of the product's perceived and real value. ([4:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=247s))
- `[OPERATIONS]` 1:1s are blocked into Mon/Wed/Fri 1–5pm. Client calls and DMs double as content ideas, product-improvement signals and paid training data for the AI product. ([7:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=463s))
- `[ORGANIC]` YouTube and Instagram are discovery channels, but word of mouth closes deals. Content-only buyers can take 6–10 months to convert. The usual path: they watch, try a tactic, get a result, then buy. ([11:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=712s))
- `[ORGANIC]` Don't optimise for views. Spikes in views don't produce more applicants (he sees it as an inverse signal), and new viewers convert 3–6 months later. Lead flow is very consistent. ([14:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=866s))
- `[ORGANIC]` Real lead spikes come from getting in front of rooms of buyers who have already paid a similar amount for something similar. ([13:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=835s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` Every buyer goes through a sales call, even from organic. Both sides have questions, and the call checks fit for results and for the community. ([14:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=897s))
- `[SALES-CALL]` The rule he stresses to reps: selling is a game of expectations, because "expectations equals success". Make sure outcomes are realistic and achievable quickly, and keep expectations aligned after the sale too. ([15:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=958s))
- `[SALES-MGMT]` Commission clawback as quality control: if a rep closes the wrong person, the client is removed and refunded and the rep isn't paid. A closer who kept closing bad fits (inflating churn) was made a public example. ([17:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1020s))
- `[APPLICATION]` Write down the characteristics of the ideal client so the team can vet for them. The two biggest qualifiers are an income/revenue threshold and the type of offer the prospect runs. Finer points accumulate over time. ([18:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1114s))
- `[PRICING]` Price ladder: $1.5K → 2K → 3K → 4K → 5K → 6K → 7K → $10K/month, raised every month or two because too many people bought. At $6K he still sold about 25 annual deals in a month. $10K/month with an annual commitment plus vetting was the "sweet spot". ([19:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1145s))
- `[FUNNEL]` Deliberate friction: a name/email opt-in that isn't even saved, a "do you read?" application question, and no self-scheduling (applicants apply and the team reaches out). The aim is to get more of the wrong people to say no. ([20:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1208s))
- `[OFFER]` Real scarcity: at $946K recurring he posted an Instagram story with the numbers, said six slots were left before he capped at $1M/month ("play chicken with me"), and all six filled in under 24 hours. ([21:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1299s))
- `[OFFER]` Downsell/waitlist ladder: a "half circle" at $5K/month (no 1:1s, no in-person attendance) gets first call on open seats, then Jeremy AI members, then the general non-buyer waitlist. Half-circle members churn faster because there's no personal relationship. ([22:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1360s))
- `[KPIS]` Annualised churn on the inner circle is 3–4%. With a cap of about 250 members, that frees 1–3 seats a month to fill from the waitlist. ([28:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1730s), [33:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=2038s))
- `[PRICING]` Use the Richard Mille model when demand exceeds capacity: raise the price, say no more often, and lift the minimum revenue threshold rather than adding seats or junior coaches. ([28:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1700s))
- `[OFFER]` Give people a real reason to act now. Without one, people buy at random times that suit their own schedule. Price signalling helps, but a genuine deadline or cap is what moves them. ([32:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1943s))
- `[CASE-STUDIES]` Silver trophies for members who reach $1M, $3M and $5M a month. Winners give a talk at the mastermind, and successful members become a reflection of the brand. ([26:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1607s))
- `[APPLICATION]` He still makes room for impressive applicants even when full, and turns away people who technically qualify but have weak offers or will be high-maintenance. ([35:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=2129s))
- `[OFFER]` A low-ticket, highly scalable offer (Jeremy AI at about $1K/month, selling 5–20 a day) sits alongside the capped premium offer and acts as a feeder to it. ([7:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=432s))
**Short quotes:**
- "All they do with the extra demand they get is raise their price and tell more people no." ([28:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1730s))
- "It's more so a game of expectations than anything else because expectations equals success." ([15:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=958s))
**G&L angle:**
- Tie closer commission to fit. If a rep closes a buyer with no realistic path to a seller-financed deal, or an owner who isn't actually ready to exit, claw back the commission. Track refunds and churn by rep.
- Put hard qualifiers into the application. For buyers: liquid capital, operating experience, target deal size. For sellers: revenue/EBITDA and owner age/timeline. Add friction questions that filter out tyre-kickers.
- Lead with expectation-setting on calls: realistic timelines to a first acquisition, typical vendor-finance terms, and how long exit prep takes.
- Consider a capped, premium inner-circle tier (for example, active acquirers doing several deals) with a cheaper "half-circle" or group tier as a waitlist feeder, and use genuine cohort caps rather than fake scarcity.
- Celebrate client closings (first deal closed, exit completed) with public awards and talks. That builds the word of mouth Haynes says actually closes high-ticket buyers.

## The truth about running a business with your bros — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q
**Gist:** Most partnerships break because conditions change while terms don't. The tree analogy: heavy early contribution, later the business pays out with no contribution, and partners resent that. Partner only when each person covers a different bucket (get clients, keep clients, ops) or brings unique money or time. Otherwise go solo, or pay operators salary with renegotiable terms.
**Nuggets:**
- `[PARTNERSHIP]` Partnerships fail because conditions change and terms don't. The vibe and willingness to work shift after splitting a few million dollars ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=0s), [0:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=31s))
- `[PARTNERSHIP]` Most partners start with no contract, or a loose one, because asking feels awkward when you like the person. Get a written contract for each person's contribution anyway ([1:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=62s))
- `[PARTNERSHIP]` Tree analogy: years 1–2 need heavy contribution, years 2–4 less, and by years 5–10 the tree gives fruit for nothing. That's the ideal for a solo owner, but in a partnership one partner taking fruit without contributing feels wrong to the other ([1:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=93s), [2:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=125s), [2:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=156s))
- `[PARTNERSHIP]` No good company has two CEOs. Partners with the same interests and skills are redundant ("two people over-watering the tree"). Only partner when each brings unique skills, money or time, specifically covering a different bucket of get clients / keep clients / keep everyone out of jail (ops) with no overlap ([3:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=188s), [3:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=219s), [4:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=250s), [4:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=281s))
- `[PARTNERSHIP]` Two partnerships that worked for him. His wife: marriage keeps the core terms fixed whatever the business does. His COO: a materially different skill set, paid salary with a guaranteed floor and terms always open to renegotiation, not equity with unlimited downside ([4:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=281s), [5:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=343s))
- `[LEADERSHIP]` When Viral Coach was $500k in debt, he guaranteed the COO a salaried role in whatever came next, adjusting terms as conditions changed ([6:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=374s))
- `[PARTNERSHIP]` A 50/50 split between two broke founders with no plan for when money arrives is a time bomb, typically going off 6–12 months after real money starts. Unless the partner is very rich or highly technical in a different area, start solo, work twice as hard and keep 100% ([6:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=404s), [7:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=436s))
**Short quotes:**
- "The reason most partnerships suck is because the conditions change over time and the terms don't." ([0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=0s))
- "No good company has two CEOs." ([3:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dHCGD1Xth3Q&t=219s))
**G&L angle:**
- G&L partners (e.g. Bendik and Josh) should write down which bucket each owns (acquisition/marketing and sales vs delivery/deal execution vs ops/legal), with contribution-linked terms and scheduled renegotiation triggers such as a revenue threshold or time contributed.
- Bring operators (COO, deal lead) in on salary plus bonus with a floor rather than equity, unless they bring unique capital or skills.
- This is useful content for buyers: many G&L clients will buy with partners or investors. Teach the "conditions change, terms don't" trap and to structure shareholder agreements around roles, vesting and buy-sell clauses. It's also relevant to vendor-finance earn-outs where the seller stays on.

## Buying XP in real life — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs
**Gist:** Using a Pokémon "grind XP" analogy, Daniel describes three escalating ways to buy experience: courses and books (slowest), hiring employees (buying someone's life experience), and hiring agencies (fractional access to many experts at once). He credits front-loading all three for Viral Coach's fast start.
**Nuggets:**
- `[MINDSET]` Level 1, buy education: courses are like school, you pay and extract the useful lessons. Most of his rich online friends started by buying each other's courses. It's the slowest way to buy experience. ([0:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=32s))
- `[HIRING]` Level 2, buy an employee: for complex builds (e.g. a CRM integrating marketing, sales and website) don't learn it yourself; pay a salary to acquire someone's decades of mastery. Many founders never reach this level because they think it isn't worth six figures for something they could do themselves. ([1:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=94s))
- `[LIFE]` Once you make good money, outsource housekeeping, laundry and cooking to free hours for bigger problems. The point isn't whether you *can* do it. ([2:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=154s))
- `[OPERATIONS]` Viral Coach day one: bought a business book series that all employees read, started with three employees, and hired an agency. ([3:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=185s))
- `[HIRING]` Level 3, hire an agency: you get ~10–12 people's fractional expertise (tech integrations, tagging, Meta pixel, etc.) for roughly the cost of one to one and a half salaries. This is how his CMO (zero marketing experience, economics degree) ramped fast when courses and a single consultant weren't enough. ([3:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=217s))
- `[SCALING-STAGES]` Front-loading purchased experience is why Viral Coach did ~$120K in month one and ~$500K in month three, nearly $1M in 90 days. Month two is garbled in the captions. ([5:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=343s))
- `[HIRING]` When you hit a challenge, buy more experience: grab another employee "off the shelf" or hire an agency for a couple of months. They've hired about a dozen agencies so far. ([6:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=374s))
- `[OFFER]` Positioning: an agency sells clients "multiple people's worth of experience" from day one, cheaply. That's the value proposition. ([7:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=438s))
**Short quotes:**
- "It's not about doing it yourself. It's about having someone so that you don't spend your time doing that." ([2:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=154s))
- "What do you do when you come across a challenge? You just go and buy more experience." ([6:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=374s))
**G&L angle:**
- Don't build the funnel, CRM, pixel/CAPI tracking and ad creative from scratch alone. Hire a specialist paid-ads/funnel agency for the first months to compress the learning curve, then bring it in-house.
- Hire experienced people (an M&A lawyer, a deal analyst, an experienced high-ticket sales manager) instead of learning every function yourself.
- Position G&L itself as "buying experience": clients get a team (advisor, lawyer, accountant, finance network) that has done many deals, instead of learning acquisitions alone over years.

## The disappointment of making $5 million per month — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4
**Gist:** Daniel reveals the single change behind the $3M→$5M jump: instead of emailing support to refund during the trial, clients had to tell their account manager on a recorded call. His real point is that such one-line unlocks are business-specific, take enormous context to find, and are best shortcut by learning from someone who has solved the same problem.
**Nuggets:**
- `[GUARANTEE]` The unlock: refunds within the trial window must be requested by telling the account manager on a recorded call, not by emailing support ("that and a couple of other details"). ([1:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=94s))
- `[FULFILLMENT]` Why it works: clients hire you for accountability, not information (Planet Fitness in-person cancellation; personal trainer). Holding them to what they said they wanted — via an assigned account manager and a call — turned retention around. ([2:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=160s))
- `[MINDSET]` A single small change can be worth ~$2M — but it took ~a year stuck, millions in ads and fixing a thousand other things to find it; the context matters more than the hack. ([1:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=63s))
- `[MINDSET]` At scale, outcomes stop being linear with hours (unlike billable accounting work) — progress comes from finding one or two key decisions; exploring and taking risks finds "excavator keys." ([4:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=254s))
- `[PARTNERSHIP]` Expertise is local: he only advises/partners with marketing companies ($1–30M/year, with product-market fit), not biz-op, brick-and-mortar or e-com, because he's solved those exact problems at $500k, $1M, $3M and $5M/month. ([5:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=348s))
- `[MINDSET]` Learning a solved problem is like learning a bike — months the first time, seconds after; pay someone who's ridden it rather than learn by falling. ([6:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=411s))
**Short quotes:**
- "The limiting thing is not how to do the bench press, it is the accountability." ([3:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=192s))
- "It's not going to be one small change that fixes your business, even though it was one small change for mine." ([1:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=94s))
**G&L angle:**
- If G&L has any refund/cooling-off clause, require cancellation to be discussed live with the client's assigned advisor on a recorded call — a chance to re-commit them to their acquisition/exit goals.
